Machine Learning to enhance student performance: A comprehensive review of higher education applications

Ricardo Miguel Santos, Roberto Henriques

*NOVA Information Management School (NOVA IMS), Universidade Nova de Lisboa, 1070-312 Lisboa, Portugal*

# Abstract

Higher education often poses substantial challenges for students, with many facing barriers that lead to delayed graduation or program withdrawal. This systematic literature review examines the role of machine learning (ML) applications in addressing these challenges by identifying at-risk students and improving academic outcomes. The study focuses on four core tasks: predictive modelling, descriptive modelling, knowledge tracing (KT), and education recommender systems (ERS). Through a review of 285 peer-reviewed papers, we identify how learners are represented (as aggregate, dynamic, and sequence-dependent) and analyse how these representations are used across diverse educational tasks. While Learning Management System (LMS) logs are the most widely used data source, integrating them with other sources consistently improves model performance and potential actionability. KT benefits from dynamic and sequence-dependent representations to model students' evolving knowledge states, whereas predictive modelling uses aggregate and dynamic representations for early warnings and performance forecasts. Descriptive modelling uncovers learning strategies using clustering techniques, while ERS delivers personalised recommendations through adaptive representations and reinforcement learning. Despite the progress, challenges remain in multimodal data integration, ensuring early-stage actionability, integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) into the current workflows and achieving cross-context model portability.

*Keywords:* Learning management systems, Higher education, Machine learning, Student performance, Learning Analytics

*Email address:* rcsantos@novaims.unl.pt (Ricardo Miguel Santos)

# Introduction

Higher education is the natural next step for students who have finished high school and wish to pursue high-paying jobs and a better life for themselves (Goulart et al., 2022). This aspiration drives a growing demand for college degrees, with many nations explicitly targeting an increase in the number of graduates entering the workforce (Alexander et al., 2019). However, the transition from secondary education to higher education is often an insurmountable barrier for many students (Hao et al., 2015). As a result, numerous students struggle with their coursework, delaying entry into the labour market or abandoning their career aspirations altogether (Von Hippel & Hofflinger, 2021).

Providing students with adequate support is essential to improving retention and ensuring successful outcomes (Bernacki et al., 2020; Macfadyen & Dawson, 2010). However, delivering this support is fraught with technical and logistical challenges. Technically, effectively modelling the learning process is complex (Corbett & Anderson, 1995). At its core, learning can be conceptualised as a dynamic process where students, starting from a specific knowledge state, interact with their educational environments to build and update their understanding (Corbett & Anderson, 1995; Yin et al., 2023). This process is influenced by prior experiences, cognitive strategies, and engagement with learning materials, making it highly individualised (*Knowing What Students Know*, 2001; Mislevy et al., 2003). Consequently, meaningful support must address each student's unique needs, prompting critical questions: *how can educators identify a student who is struggling? What content is the student struggling with? More importantly, how can these issues be identified early enough to provide timely intervention*?

Answering these questions is pivotal for improving educational outcomes through early intervention (Baneres et al., 2019; Bernacki et al., 2020). However, implementing effective support strategies is logistically demanding. In instructor-paced settings like higher education, assessments often culminate in final exams, where learning inadequacies are revealed too late for corrective action (Hu et al., 2014; Macfadyen & Dawson, 2010). Intermediate assessments, such as tests or deliverables, could offer earlier insights into student performance. Yet, these solutions either burden already overextended instructors or require students to fail before being eligible to receive help (Bernacki et al., 2020). These constraints tend to result in students only receiving feedback when there is no time to make meaningful corrections that could have prevented failure (Hu et al., 2014; Riestra-González et al., 2021). Thus, finding new and better ways to represent students without additional workload would be a win-win situation.

Over the years, our ability to collect and use behavioural data from multiple sources has grown massively (Sharif & Uckelmann, 2024) and, along with it, so has the body of research in learning analytics (Cerezo et al., 2024). A key driver of this growth is the widespread adoption of learning management systems (LMS) in higher education (Coates et al., 2005). LMS logs can be interpreted as an unobtrusive and abundant source of data that reflects, albeit incompletely, student engagement with learning content (Baker et al., 2020; Cerezo et al., 2016). To address the limitations of LMS logs, researchers have integrated data from complementary sources, such as surveys (Gašević et al., 2017), student demographics (Gašević et al., 2016), historical performance (Asif et al., 2017), video (Elbawab & Henriques, 2023) or even multimodal approaches that combine data from multiple sources (A. Chen et al., 2025; Sharif & Uckelmann, 2024). Nowadays, learning analytics research encompasses a myriad of different applications. These range from merely using data to understand the processes by which learners learn (Tempelaar et al., 2024) to develop machine learning (ML) models that can identify students in need of support (R. Santos & Henriques, 2023) or recommending content that is directed towards a learner's needs and interests (Wan et al., 2022).

However, the growth of learning analytics and its sheer number of problems and approaches led to a certain *muddying of the waters* about what the field entails (Lang et al., 2022). Even within the narrowed focus of improving learning outcomes in higher education, addressing this problem effectively requires multiple narrower tasks (Romero & Ventura, 2020) and, within each task, the literature features significant variations regarding problem definition and data/models used (Lang et al., 2017). In this work, we identify four distinct learning analytical tasks that together address the technical (learner representation) and logistical (identification and intervention) challenges educators face in higher education: Predictive Modelling, Descriptive Modelling, Knowledge Tracing (KT) and Educational Recommender Systems (ERS). When aligned to improve student outcomes, these tasks can create better learner representations, which leads to better identification of student needs and, consequently, more effective personalised interventions. However, while there is an overarching goal, the literature explores these tasks in isolation (A. Khan & Ghosh, 2021; Maphosa & Maphosa, 2023; Shen et al., 2024). Thus, there is a notable gap in integrating these approaches into a cohesive framework that simultaneously reduces educators' workload and maximises benefits for students, ultimately enhancing outcomes in higher education.

In this work, we take a step towards building this framework by reviewing the learning analytics literature on ML applications aimed at enhancing learner success in higher education. Under this perspective, we identify the key methodologies, data sources, and models across four main dimensions: predicting student success, describing student behaviour, KT and ERS. Specifically, we aim to answer the following questions:

1. What are the common sources of student data, and how can these different sources be effectively combined?

2. For each task, what are the primary applications and their most effective learner representations?

3. What are the most effective educational ML models for each task, and how are they evaluated?

To answer these research questions, we performed a systematic literature review by adapting the Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses (PRISMA) guidelines (Page, McKenzie, et al., 2021). We have reviewed 285 research papers published between 2012 and 2026 in peer-reviewed academic journals and conference proceedings associated with data mining, machine learning or learning analytics. Under very strict circumstances, we also considered ArXiv papers.

The remainder of this work is organised as follows: the next chapter presents our search and paper selection methodology. The third chapter presents the main findings of the reviewed works, while the fourth discusses them in the context of the research questions. The fifth and final chapter concludes by outlining the work's most significant implications, limitations and future research directions.

# Methodology

Our workflow follows an adaptation of the PRISMA 2020 guidelines (Page, Moher, et al., 2021) using the Population/Intervention/Control/Outcome (PICO) framework. We focused on ML applications in higher education, specifically addressing the four learning analytics tasks outlined in the introduction: Predictive Modelling, Descriptive Modelling, KT and ERS. The whole process, expanded in more detail throughout the following subsections, is summarised in Figure 1.

**Figure 1 –** Summary of the selection process. Unique count refers to the number of research papers accepted for a given task. Task-specific counting of selected papers is 301 because it considers overlaps (e.g. Riestra-González et al. (2021) was selected for both predictive and descriptive modelling tasks.

* 1. *Screening criteria under the PICO framework*

Before conducting the literature search, we assessed the eligibility of potential research papers using the PICO framework (Table I). Our main inclusion criteria targeted English-written works published in peer-reviewed journals, international conferences and ArXiv pre-prints that (i) focus on higher education students, Massive Open Online Courses (MOOC) or Intelligent Tutoring Systems (ITS); and (ii) propose or evaluate ML methods relevant to at least one of the four learning analytics tasks.

**Table I** – Eligibility criteria of research papers using the PICO framework

|  |  |
| --- | --- |
| **Component** | **Criteria** |
| **Population** | ITS, MOOCs, Courses, or programs aimed at higher education students. |
| **Intervention** | The study must employ ML techniques for at least one of the four learning analytics tasks under review (Predictive Modelling, Descriptive Modelling, KT or ERS).  Moreover, it must incorporate or propose a novel model, methodology, or data representation strategy that has the potential to enhance student learning outcomes. |
| **Comparison** | The paper should compare the proposed ML approach against established baselines, conventional methods, or previously published results. If no direct comparison is made, the study must provide evidence (e.g., statistical tests) supporting its approach. |
| **Outcome** | Must present clear, measurable outcomes or evaluation metrics (e.g., accuracy, retention, engagement) demonstrating the effectiveness of their approach. In addition, it should feasibly lead to better learner representations, improved identification of students at risk, or improved personalised interventions. |

* 1. *Search Strategy*

Between January 2023 and April 2026, we iteratively searched for research papers across Google Scholar, Web of Science, and the ACM Digital Library. During the initial iterations, we sorted the papers by citation count in descending order, examined the first 10 pages of results, and preliminarily selected potentially eligible papers based on their titles. On later iterations, we replicated this approach but filtered by year. We used multiple search strings to capture studies on higher education, learning analytics and machine learning. Typical search queries included (a full list of the 68 search strings used is available in Table A.I in Appendix A):

"Education Data Mining" AND "Higher Education" AND "Student Performance",

"Early Prediction" AND "Learning Analytics" AND "Student Performance",

"Knowledge Tracing" AND "Machine Learning",

"Academic Success" AND "Learning Management System",

To ensure that we included relevant studies not initially flagged by our search strategy, we also conducted forward and backward citation tracing of the papers we reviewed. Using this approach, we identified 442 candidate research papers, which we imported into Zotero to advance to the double-filter stage.

* 1. *Selection Process*

The inclusion or exclusion of each candidate research paper was decided using a double-filter strategy similar to the one used in López-Zambrano et al. (2021). The first filter excluded papers that violated the inclusion criteria upon abstract review (e.g., works exclusively using K-12 settings, technology adoption or purely theoretical without ML components). Papers that passed the first filter underwent a full-text review. Those failing to meet the criteria (e.g., not displaying any form of evaluation or not having an, at the very least, implied baseline for comparison) were excluded at this stage. This procedure culminated in 285 papers deemed eligible for review.

* 1. *Labelling and Task Assignment*

After being accepted for review, each paper was labelled according to the primary learning analytics task(s) it addressed: Predictive Modelling, Descriptive Modelling, KT or ERS. In cases where a paper included more than one task, we assigned multiple labels.

# Results

This section examines similarities and differences in the literature and is divided into two main subsections. In the first subsection, we analyse the inputs that power machine learning models and how they are converted into features or sequences suitable for ML algorithms and statistical tools. We emphasise the prominence of LMS data, the growing trend toward hybrid data integration, and the variety of representation methods. The second subsection then explores each of the four learning analytics in detail: Predictive Modelling, Descriptive Modelling, KT, and ERS. For each, we examine its practical uses, main modelling techniques, the current state of the art, and potential challenges.

* 1. *Data sources and learner representations*

A common saying in ML is *Garbage in, garbage out*. This expression emphasises that using well-curated and preprocessed data increases the likelihood of building better models. Conversely, poor-quality data paired with advanced algorithms is unlikely to produce good results. Good quality educational data should be able to represent a learner as best as possible, but it is difficult to come by (Macfadyen & Dawson, 2010).

As shown in Table II, LMS, Virtual Learning Environment (VLE), and MOOC logs are the most frequently used data sources in the reviewed works, appearing either as general platform logs or as specific subsets, including forum activity, submissions, or answer sequences in ITS. Logs do not provide a complete view of learners' behaviours, motivations, or knowledge, but have become particularly valuable because they unobtrusively and continuously record interactions between learners and their virtual environment and resources (Cerezo et al., 2016; Riestra-González et al., 2021; Wan & Niu, 2016). The boundaries between data source categories are not always well-defined. While answer sequences, submission traces, forum activity, and exercise-level interactions technically originate from system logs, we coded these attributes separately to treat them as distinct analytical objects rather than generic LMS/VLE/MOOC logs. For example, an answer sequence in KT is not just a broad indicator of platform engagement, but the primary structure through which the model estimates changes in mastery over time. Similarly, exercise metadata and knowledge components are not merely contextual information; they define the items, concepts, or skills around which knowledge is modelled. This distinction is particularly important for KT and ERS, where question/answer interactions and sequences are often key to the modelling task rather than just supplementary log-derived data (Badrinath & Pardos, 2025; Huo et al., 2020).

While logs are prevalent across all tasks, Table II also shows that the data sources used vary by analytical task. Predictive modelling studies more frequently combine logs with assessment records, demographic variables, academic background information, or administrative data (Gašević et al., 2016; Nimy et al., 2023; Romero, López, et al., 2013). Descriptive modelling studies, in turn, make greater use of questionnaires, interviews, surveys, and other sources that help contextualise or interpret learner behaviour (Gašević et al., 2017; L.-Y. Li & Tsai, 2017; Srivastava et al., 2022). Exercise or question metadata, answer sequences or knowledge components are more common in KT and ERS (C.-Q. Huang et al., 2024; Y. Wang et al., 2025) than in the remaining tasks (S. N. Liao et al., 2019).

Within this broader landscape, many studies have shown the value of integrating additional data streams to improve the representations of learners and their learning processes (Adejo & Connolly, 2018; Butt et al., 2023). Regardless of the task, multi-modal approaches provide a more comprehensive view of learners, often resulting in improved performance compared with the exclusive use of log data. For example, hybrid predictive models that combine LMS features with student characteristics have surpassed baseline models that consider only student characteristics (Gašević et al., 2016; Sandoval et al., 2018) or LMS clicks in isolation (Adejo & Connolly, 2018; Waheed et al., 2020; R. Yu et al., 2020). In KT, works like Zhao et al., (2020) have looked to complement the traditional use of answer sequences with behavioural logs. Thus, educational modelling is moving beyond readily available interaction traces toward more heterogeneous representations of learners, tasks, and learning environments.

Once the data are assembled, researchers must decide how learners will be represented: that is, how student information is encoded and structured for subsequent analysis. In the reviewed literature, we identified three broad forms of learner representation: static aggregate representations, sequence-based representations, and dynamic representations. As shown in Figure 2, static aggregate representations are the most frequently used; sequence-based representations have become increasingly prevalent in recent years; and dynamic representations, although less common overall, play an important role in KT and ERS (Figure 3). Importantly, data sources and learner representations are related but distinct dimensions of model design. Depending on the techniques used, data from the same source can be manipulated into any of these representations. Thus, the prevalence of a specific data source does not necessarily imply a single way of modelling the learner.

The most common approach is the static aggregate representation, in which a student’s logs, demographic characteristics, assessment records, or prior records are summarised into a single, snapshot-like, time-agnostic vector that describes the learner over a predefined observation window (Cerezo et al., 2016; Conijn et al., 2017; Riestra-González et al., 2021). For example, a learner may be represented by the total number of clicks, forum posts, prior grade point average (GPA), or demographic attributes, thereby compressing or ignoring the temporal order of events and describing the learner through summarised indicators (Baker et al., 2020).

As highlighted in Figure 3, static aggregate representations are particularly popular and effective for both Predictive and Descriptive modelling tasks that aim to classify or cluster learners at specific time points using human-interpretable variables (Hu et al., 2014; Riestra-González et al., 2021). Moreover, this representation is convenient because its simplicity allows the integration of data from multiple sources, it is well-suited to standard statistical and ML models, and it is easy to interpret. However, its simplicity is also its main limitation. Since it provides a compressed view of the learner, a static representation does not capture a learner’s progress over time. When used for longitudinal monitoring, researchers often need to construct separate feature sets or models for different prediction windows, which can limit their usefulness for real-time or adaptive applications (Conijn et al., 2017; Riestra-González et al., 2021; R. Santos & Henriques, 2023).

![The graph shows a downward trend in the number of papers using static learner representations from 2012 to 2024, while dynamic and sequential representations have seen a steady increase.  AI-generated content may be incorrect.](data:image/png;base64...)

**Figure 2 –** Learner representation usage over time. Static aggregate representations are the most frequently used representation type across the reviewed literature. Sequence-based representations become increasingly common from the late 2010s onward, while dynamic representations remain less frequent overall, despite gaining visibility in recent years.

A second form is the sequence-based representation, which treats student data as a multivariate time series. Instead of summarising behaviours in a single vector, learning is represented as an ordered trajectory of actions, which makes it especially useful, as a learning process can be conceived as a sequence of events (Baker et al., 2020). This form of representation has been especially prominent in KT and ERS, as shown in Figure 3. In KT, answer sequences of Multiple Choice Questions (MCQ) are often the default representation because the goal is to model how a learner’s knowledge states evolve across different knowledge components (Pardos et al., 2013; Piech et al., 2015). Historically, in Predictive or Descriptive Modelling tasks, this form of representation has been less common (Baker et al., 2020). Still, while shallow, there is precedent on the use of Markov Chains and Hidden Markov Models for both predictive (Brooks et al., 2015; Mahzoon et al., 2018) and descriptive tasks (Moubayed et al., 2020). More recently, however, the increasing popularity of deep learning techniques like Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs), Long Short-Term Memory Units (LSTMs), and Transformer-based architectures has made sequence-based representations more effective in KT, ERS and Predictive Modelling studies (Deng et al., 2025; Hegde et al., 2024; Waheed et al., 2023).

The third form is the dynamic representation, in which a learner’s profile is continuously updated as new interactions occur (Corbett & Anderson, 1995). Dynamic representations of learners typically begin as multivariate vectors, similar to those in static aggregate models. However, these vectors are constantly updated through interactions with various content, learning materials, or other learners. Unlike sequence-based representations, which track a series of changes, dynamic representations explicitly revise the learner profile or knowledge state as new evidence appears, without retaining the sequence of those changes. Although they are less common than static or sequence-based approaches in the reviewed literature, dynamic models are conceptually vital for tasks that require ongoing adaptation, such as those in KT. For example, Dynamic Key-Value Memory Networks (DKVMN) utilise a static memory matrix for exercise information and a dynamic one to track a learner’s aptitude across various KCs. (X. Sun et al., 2022; J. Zhang et al., 2017). Dynamic representations are also popular among different forms of ERS, be they traditional content-based filtering (Wan & Niu, 2016), collaborative filtering (Dascalu et al., 2015) or hybrid filtering ERS (Wan & Niu, 2020).

![The image depicts a comparison of various learning models (Predictive, Descriptive, and Knowledge Tracing) and their number of publications over a span of years from 2012 to 2026.  AI-generated content may be incorrect.](data:image/png;base64...)

**Figure 3 –** Learner representation usage over time, separated by task. Static Aggregate representations have dominated Predictive and Descriptive Modelling. Sequence and Dynamic representations are prominent in KT and ERS.

* 1. *Learning Analytics Tasks*
     1. *Predictive Modelling*

Within this work, predictive modelling refers to the use of supervised machine learning techniques to operationalise student success and identify students at risk of underperformance or dropout. A central challenge in this area is that “risk” is not defined consistently across the literature, with studies differing in both the educational outcome they predict and the methodological conditions under which prediction is attempted (Mahzoon et al., 2018; Riestra-González et al., 2021; Whitehill et al., 2017). At the level of problem definition, the main distinction concerns the prediction horizon: some studies focus on short-term success (e.g., performance at the end of a curricular unit), whereas others focus on longer-term outcomes (e.g., dropout, progression, or on-time graduation in a program). At the methodological level, the key distinction concerns actionability: whether predictions are made early enough to support intervention or are primarily retrospective assessments of model performance.

Table III summarises these variations by classifying studies according to prediction horizon, ML task, target variable, early intervention potential, and interventions used (the complete literature review table of the works discussed herein is presented in Appendix B’s Table B.I). Most studies focus on short-term classification tasks to identify students at risk of failure based on course or exam performance. Regression tasks are also present, involving continuous indicators like grades, exam scores or GPA. The table highlights a persistent imbalance: many studies make early predictions, but fewer link these to interventions, systems, or support. Predictive models can be technically valid without showing educational impact. A model might predict student performance well but remain disconnected from the pedagogical or institutional processes needed to support students.

* + - 1. *What counts as student performance and success?*

One of the main difficulties in interpreting predictive modelling studies is that they use very different definitions of student performance (R. Santos & Henriques, 2023). On the one hand, multiple works view performance as a short-term endeavour: performance at the end of a specific course (Bernacki et al., 2020; Sonnleitner et al., 2025; Zacharis, 2018; F. Zhang et al., 2023), whether the student drops out of a course (Coussement et al., 2020; Whitehill et al., 2017), success at the next specific assignment or activity (Azcona et al., 2018; Divjak et al., 2024; Doleck et al., 2020; Nimy et al., 2023) or even more specific elements of student behaviour, such as the quality of the next interaction (Dias et al., 2020) or whether the student exhibits procrastinating behaviour based on how early the student submits an assignment relative to its deadline (Imhof et al., 2023). On the other hand, student performance can also be defined as a long-term process: whether the student will graduate or not within a program’s expected timeline (Agrusti et al., 2020; Beaulac & Rosenthal, 2019), the grade the student may get in an important program-defining exam (Mastour et al., 2023), end-of-program indicators such as graduation GPA (Y. Chen et al., 2023; Z. Khan et al., 2024) or even whether a student will proceed to post-graduate studies after completing the current undergraduate program (Iatrellis et al., 2021). Most of the reviewed works either follow one approach or the other, with only a handful attempting to predict both short- and long-term success (Daud et al., 2017; Mahzoon et al., 2018; R. Yu et al., 2020).

# Discussion

* 1. *Research Question 1: What are the common sources of student data, and how is this information combined?*

LMS data is the most commonly used source in educational applications, capturing student interactions such as logins, submissions, and resource views. While widely accessible and unobtrusive, LMS data alone often lacks the depth needed for nuanced analysis. To improve performance and generate more comprehensive learner representations, researchers combine it with additional data, including demographics, prior academic records, survey results, and timestamped events. Integrating these data sources enhances the ability to accurately identify students in need and provide actionable insights. Multimodal approaches, combining data from multiple sources, have also been shown to consistently outperform single-source models on multiple tasks.

However, this does not come without its challenges. One of the main concerns about educational data centres is actionability: a student needs to be correctly identified as needing help before it is too late to make a correction. This problem is often manifested by an absence of student data at earlier stages of a course or program. Successful integration relies on aligning the granularity and temporal scope of data while ensuring scalability.

* 1. *Research Question 2: For each task, what are the primary applications and their most effective learner representations?*

For KT, the primary application is modeling a student's evolving knowledge state to predict future performance and guide instructional decisions. The most effective learner representations are dynamic representations, which dynamically adjust to changes in knowledge states as students interact with learning materials and are that are a cornerstone of BKT. Sequence-dependent representations also a role to play in KT, especially since the rise in popularity of Deep Knowledge Tracing, whether it is using RNN or attention-based models.

For predictive modeling, the main applications are the prediction of academic performance, whether it is at the end of a single course or the end of a program. This task can be additionally disentangled into identification of students at risk, whether it is using data collected until the end of a course or an early warning systems. Effective representations depend on the task. Aggregate non-temporal representations are commonly used for end-of-course predictions. For early warning systems, but there is a role to be played by both dynamic and sequence-dependent representations.

For descriptive modeling, the goal is to identify student learning strategies and their connections to academic outcomes. Aggregate non-temporal representations are effective here, particularly for clustering students into groups based on behaviour and engagement patterns. These representations, while less temporally detailed, provide insights into how specific strategies, such as consistent engagement or distributed study time, correlate with academic success.

Finally, for ERS, applications include recommending tailored learning materials, exercises, or courses. Dynamic representations are most effective because they adapt to changes in students' behaviour and knowledge over time, enabling personalised recommendations. These representations, combined with reinforcement learning, neural networks, or self-organising maps, support continuous refinement of the recommendations to align with student progress.

# Conclusion

This systematic literature review aimed to synthesize the learning analytics research and showcase the potential of its applications to improve student outcomes in higher education. By leveraging diverse sources of student data, such as LMS logs, demographics, and historical performance, researchers have developed advanced models and representations tailored to specific tasks, including knowledge tracing, predictive modelling, descriptive modelling, and education recommender systems. Effective learner representations (whether aggregate, dynamic, or sequence-dependent) play a pivotal role in enhancing the accuracy and relevance of these applications.

However, several challenges persist. The integration of multimodal data sources, while promising, faces obstacles such as early-stage data availability and scalability. Additionally, ensuring timely and actionable insights requires models that balance predictive accuracy with early identification capabilities. Research gaps remain in standardizing data integration frameworks and optimizing model portability across varied educational contexts.

Future work should address these challenges by refining learner representations, developing adaptive systems that respond to real-time student needs, and exploring the ethical implications of data-driven educational tools. By addressing these gaps, the field can move closer to creating equitable, effective, and scalable solutions that support student success in higher education.

In conclusion, learning analytics is a vibrant research discipline for tackling the perpetual challenge of identifying and supporting struggling students before barriers to success become insurmountable. By enhancing predictive models, broadening the evidence base across contexts, integrating with institutional processes, and embracing new data streams, the field can make major strides toward more efficient and equitable higher education for all learners. The potential societal impacts profoundly open doors to socioeconomic mobility and facilitate a skilled 21st century workforce. With sustained innovation, these data-driven approaches can fundamentally reshape how we approach student engagement and success.

# Appendix B. Literature review tables

Across our work, we have identified 285 research papers that met the selection criteria outlined in section 2.1. This Appendix summarises the key attributes of each research work covered in our literature review. Tables B.I., B.II., B.III. and B.IV summarise the research works reviewed for Predictive Modelling, Descriptive Modelling, KT and ERS, respectively. Each table refers only to portions of the works that directly address the tasks being reviewed. Consequently, our summary may not cover the entire body of work presented in each specific paper for any given reference.

| **Study** | **Learner Representation** | **Validation** | **Data Sources** | **Theoretical Grounding** | **Sample / Setting** | **Analytic Task** | **Algorithm** | **Results/ Patterns** | **Takeaways/ Implications** | **Limitations** |
| Wolff et al. (2013) | Static Aggregate | Public Dataset | LMS/VLE/MOOC logs; Assessment/performance records; Administrative/SIS records | None Specified | 4397 - Module A 1292 - Module B 2012 - Module C - 3 OU modules (anonymised) | Association Rule Mining | General Unary Hypotheses Automaton (GUHA) (70% Confidence and 0.001 Support) | GUHA confidently identified students who failed TMAs 4, 6 and 7.   Authors do not deeply describe the obtained rules. | LMS data is hard to interpret alone and should consider module structure and intended use.   Model can be trained for module-failure prediction. | Limited algorithm comparison.  The feature set appears relatively simple. |
| Agudo-Peregrina et al. (2014) | Static Aggregate | Backtested on Historical Students | LMS/VLE/MOOC logs | Moore’s Typology of Interaction Types | 138 Online students 218 Face-to-face students - 6 online courses 2 VLE-supported F2F courses | Regression | Linear Regression | Compares various Log classification methods to identify the best predictor of performance.  Performance in face-to-face courses isn't predicted by LMS variables.  In online courses, all variables related to Agent, Mode, and Frequency were significant at alpha=0.05, except Transmission, which was not significant at 0.01. | Need to study moderating effects of interactions in online courses.   Additional data sources are needed to predict face-to-face performance.  Course creators should redesign courses to promote effective interactions and correct undesired behaviours. | Exclusive use of LMS logs.  Effective only for online contexts, not VLE-supported face-to-face.  Requires validation in more educational settings.  Interactions are evaluated only by type, not by content. |
| Bannert et al. (2014) | Sequence | Tested with New Students | Wearable devices/sensors; LMS/VLE/MOOC logs | Wynne’s SRL Model | 38 - Individualised Sessions | Process Mining | Fuzzy Miner | 2 Groups created apriori (successful vs less successful).  Model explains 40% of the sequences that were observed in the logs.  More successful students engaged more often in SRL-related processes.   Less successful students had READ into MONITOR or READ into REPEAT.  The fitness of the successful is marginally better than that of the less successful. | Authors study 38 students' SRL through sessions where they Think-Aloud, then test and classify SRL activities.  Theoretical foundations of process mining aid education, but low fitness differences indicate it struggles with the data's granularity. | Small sample size.  Lacks standardized routines for Process Mining in education, focusing on process logic but ignoring elements of the processing environment.  Process Mining often isn't suitable for statistical testing. |
| Castillo-Merino & Serradell-López (2014) | Static Aggregate | Backtested on Historical Students | Questionnaire/interview data | Theoretical Model Proposed by the Authors | 127 - 3 courses: Introduction to Financial Accounting, Introduction to Mathematics | Factor Analysis | Structural Equation Modelling | Motivation is the key predictor of performance (coefficient=0.298) and the only significant variable at 0.01.  Older students proficient in ICXT technologies are more confident in their success.  Chi-Square statistic: 57.770 with 47 degrees of Freedom. | SEM is a good model to explain student performance.   Motivation predicts success in online learning. Age and Semesters indirectly affect student performance.  Schooling, ICT, teaching methods, and Feedback intensity were deemed unimportant. | Exclusively uses questionnaire data.  Does not consider the organisational structure of the university.  Courses are taught uniformly, so no differences in teaching methods or ICT use were observed. |
| Brouwer et al. (2016) | Static Aggregate | Tested with New Students | Survey data; Academic background records | Pintrich’s SRL Model | 364 first-year students - All courses in the Behavioural and Social Sciences Department | Regression | Multi-Level Regression Analysis | 3 Groups created apriori (low achievers vs average achievers vs high achievers).  Peer and Faculty capital explain success. Family support was not relevant here. Being in a small group with similar peers isn't beneficial for low or average students.  Social circles and friendships impact high achievers more. Early success predicts later achievement.  Model significantly describes student performance. | Authors argue students lacking family support often don't reach undergraduate level.  Social interactions benefit high achievers more than lower achievers.  This model can an effective early-warning system.  Fostering social connections across levels helps low achievers access social capital to improve performance. | Study focuses only on social capital access via the number of friends, not on social networks.  Use of self-reports to measure support without any direct behavioural data.   Homogeneous sample of Behavioural and Social Sciences students. |
| Cerezo et al. (2016) | Static Aggregate | Backtested on Historical Students | LMS/VLE/MOOC logs; Assessment/performance records | None Specified | 140 students - 1 module as part of an eTRAL course | Clustering | Expectation-Maximization;  k-Means | 4 clusters:  • Cluster 1:  Non-Task or Theory-Oriented Group (non-procrastinators); • Cluster 2:  Task-Oriented Group (socially focused); • Cluster 3: Task-Oriented Group (individually focused);  • Cluster 4:  Non-Task-Oriented Group (procrastinators).  Clusters 1 and 4 are similar in task focus, as are Clusters 2 and 3. Clusters 2 and 3 show the highest average performance. | Student interactions can identify at-risk students.   Variables correlated positively with performance, except for Time Theory and Time Tasks.  Likewise, the time variables were negatively correlated with Days-hand-in.  Task-oriented student behaviour can inform recommender systems. | Does not explain why students with different engagement patterns get similar performance.  Lack of additional data sources.  Small, homogeneous sample of 3rd year psychology students. |
| Hao et al. (2016) | Static Aggregate | Backtested on Historical Students | Survey data; Assessment/performance records | None Specified | 203 students - 162 in introductory course 42 in advanced course | Regression | Linear Regression | 2 Groups created apriori (Novice vs Expert students).  Predictors of Online Searching included Academic Performance, Learning Proficiency, Epistemological Belief, and Problem Difficulty at p<0.001.  Predictors of Help-Seeking were Interest and Problem Difficulty at p<0.001.   Online help-seeking-results: R²: 0.299 - 4 factors model; Ad-R²: 0.285 - 4 factors model  Help-seeking results:  R²: 0.192 - 2 factors model, Adjusted R²: 0.184 - 4 factors model. | Expert students are more willing to seek help independently (online or offline).  Novice students need help developing online help-seeking skills because they often lack the confidence to approach professors.  Fostering online help-seeking training would benefit students and promote acceptance of independent learning. | Data is only based on self-reports.  Small sample size (even though the model assessment strategy is adequate). |
| You (2016) | Static Aggregate | Backtested on Historical Students | LMS/VLE/MOOC logs; Assessment/performance records | Pintrich’s SRL Model | 530 students - 1 Introduction to Colour Course | Regression | Hierarchical Multiple Regression | Regular study was a key predictor of student success, especially when paired with regularity, engagement, and motivation.  R²: 0.581 - 5 variable Regression in Final Course Score  R²: 0.371 - 5 variable Regression in Course Exam Score | Not all clicks are equal; assign different weights to actions and their qualities.  Creating situations that promote regular learner engagement benefits development.  Relevant variables for Final Course Score and Course Exam Score differ. | No model assessment strategy.  Could test more variables related to regularity, engagement, and motivation in online learning.  Small sample size. |
| Asif et al. (2017) | Static Aggregate | Backtested on Historical Students | Academic background records; Assessment/performance records | None Specified | 106 students for training data 104 students for test data - 1 Information Technology Program | Clustering | x-Means | 3 clusters show progression in the program: Low, intermediate, and high performers tend to remain in their performance levels over time. | Trajectory analysis shows good students stay good, and struggling students continue to struggle.   These results enable pragmatic policies to identify at-risk students.   Excluding admission marks limits early intervention.   Pathway is defined by a tuple of cluster labels for each year. | Clustering of progression does not consider pre- admission data.  Some clusters have a sample size of 1. |
| Corrin et al. (2017) | Static Aggregate | Backtested on Historical Students | LMS/VLE/MOOC logs; Course/content metadata | None Specified | 2439 learners - 4 MOOCs from University of Melbourne | Clustering | Affinity Propagation | 5 clusters:  • Cluster 1 (Low Engagement): Low grades, low activities, no forum threads/posts. • Cluster 2 (Assessment-Focused): Low grades, high assessment attempts, low video interactions and low pageviews. • Cluster 3 (Passive Engagement): Medium grades, moderate pageviews, low activity on forum. • Cluster 4 (Active Engagement): High engagement, moderate assessment attempts, high forum participation. • Cluster 5 (Assessment-focused - high grades):  High grades, high assessment attempts. | Authors use Affinity Propagation to identify help-seeking patterns from MOOC data.  MOOC designers should make forum and content interactions more engaging to prevent passive use. | Does not consider student information. |
| Gašević et al. (2017) | Static Aggregate | Tested with New Students | Questionnaire/interview data; LMS/VLE/MOOC logs; Assessment/performance records | Bigg’s 3P Model | 144 students - 1 First year Engineering Course | Clustering | Agglomerative Clustering-Ward Method | 4 clusters:  Clusters 1 and 2 are more active than 3 and 4, with students in cluster 1 engaging in more meaningful deep learning activities.  Clusters 3 and 4 have a surface-level approach, with cluster 4 less active and cluster 3 active during summative assessments.   Unsurprisingly, clusters 1 and 2 tend to perform better. | Clustering students creates 4 clusters, which are subsequently grouped into 2 groups (deep learners vs surface learners).  Differences between groups appeared in deep approach and deep strategy. However, logs do not distinguish motivation subscales or identify specific surface approaches.  Mapping SPQ answer approaches shows that deep learners score much higher on the DA scale.  Professors can deliver content that promotes deep learning through flipped classrooms and active learning. | The questionnaire was administered at the start of the course.  Susceptible to self-selection bias.  Students who answered are more active and more likely to invest in their course.  Small sample size. |
| Kizilcec et al. (2017) | Static Aggregate | Tested with New Students | Survey data | None Specified | 4831 MOOC learners - 6 Distinct MOOCs | Regression | Elastic-Net Regression | SRL constructs vary based on motivation to enter the MOOC and prior academic background.  Goal Setting and Strategic Planning are key for MOOC success.  Certificate, Meet People, Prestige are key motivators for higher SRL skills. | Learners with strong SRL skills tend to revisit course content to retest their learning.  Help-seeking is negatively linked to success, surprising given literature that shows success when learners are motivated to work with others. In online settings, communities often lack structured support systems to promote this environment. | Lasso Regression does not consider Behavioural Data.  Potential problems with the validity of the SRL constructs in the MOOC-only context. |
| Li & Tsai, (2017) | Static Aggregate | Tested with New Students | LMS/VLE/MOOC logs; Questionnaire/interview data; Assessment/performance records; Project/Code Assignments | Pintrich’s SRL Model | 59 students - 1 Mobile Phone Programming Course | Clustering | k-Means | 3 Clusters:  • Cluster 1 (n=21): More time on videos, shared assignments, posted messages - Consistent use Students  • Cluster 2 (n=19): Minimal interaction with learning resources led to poorer final exam scores compared to peers. • Cluster 3 (n=19): Slide intensive students. These take more time to consult slide lectures. | Standard clustering on LMS data maps to SRL constructs of MSLQ and final learner performance.  Students who were more engaged and willing to interact with course materials tended to outperform their peers, often due to motivational factors. | Small sample size.  A third of the students were in the less-used group.  Clusters 1 and 3 used different strategies but achieved similar exam results, indicating unaccounted factors in the data.  Not transferable to courses that contain, e.g. a textbook.  LMS data, by itself, seems to be noisy. |
| Pardo et al. (2017) | Static Aggregate | Tested with New Students | LMS/VLE/MOOC logs; Questionnaire/interview data; Assessment/performance records | None Specified | 145 first-year students - 1 Introduction to Computer Systems course | Clustering | Hierarchical Clustering | 2 Clusters:   • Cluster 1 (High self-regulated and high-achieving (n=62)): These students show the best SRL and interaction indicators and have the highest grades. • Cluster 2 (Low self-regulated and low-achieving students (n=83)):  Lowest interaction patterns and SRL values lead to the lowest grade. | Constructs and variables associated with negative performance have led to worse results.  Different sources of variables enable a more detailed assessment of student behaviour and SRL perceptions. | The survey was for the course and did not distinguish between face-to-face and online components.  SRL is dynamic, so capturing an idea at one point may not reflect the full experience across the course. |
| Regression | Linear Regression | Adj-R2: 0.32 - Model 2 | Addressing test anxiety and poor student engagement with online events may improve outcomes. Regression analysis confirms previous experiment observations. |
| Hlosta et al. (2018) | Sequence | Backtested on Historical Students | LMS/VLE/MOOC logs; Assessment/performance records | None Specified | Unspecified - 1 Course at Open University | Association Rule Mining | GUHA; Markov Model | 12 pathways:  Analyses of students with 0 LMS activity for at least one of the 4 weeks show they're at greater risk if they don’t submit for 2+ weeks. | The authors distinguish between students who succeeded in the first assignment and those who did not.  Early disconnection followed by increased activity signals success, while disconnected behaviour tends to be unsuccessful. | Undisclosed sample size.  Analysis of GUHA is limited to students with at least one week of 0 activity. |
| Howard et al. (2018) | Static Aggregate | Backtested on Historical Students | LMS/VLE/MOOC logs; Administrative/SIS records | None Specified | 144 undergraduate students - 1 Practical Statistics Course | Clustering | GMM | 3 Clusters:  • Cluster 1 (n=6): Lower total views in general and lower final result. • Cluster 2 (n=69): More views on Weekdays than on Sunday. • Cluster 3 (n=61): More views on Sunday than Weekdays, even though not by a substantial margin.  Performance differences between Cluster 2 and Cluster 3 are not visible. | Cluster shows more activity generally improves performance; however, activity levels during weekdays or weekends don't significantly differ.  Clustering via GMM is introduced as a new approach to feature generation. | Single-course data, which is unlikely to generalise well outside of STEM. |
| Stites et al. (2019) | Static Aggregate | Tested with New Students | Student demographics/characteristics; Survey data; Questionnaire/interview data | Expectancy-Value Model | 581 students - 1 Dynamics course in a US University | Clustering | k-Means | 9 Clusters  • Cluster 1: Multiple resource uses and heavy use of the tutorial room. Receptive to help. • Cluster 2: Primary reliance on core resources. • Cluster 3: Use of core resources and the tutorial room. • Cluster 4: Seek help from external students. • Cluster 5:  Did not use the blog, but used more non-course online resources. • Cluster 6: Similar to C2.  Cluster 7:  Least utilisation of the lecture book. Moderate use of other resources. • Cluster 8: Peers and lecture book usage. • Cluster 9: Infrequent use of peers. | Students seek help from multiple sources. Lecturers should spend less time advising on materials because of the variability in their use.   Authors choose the k with the second-lowest BIC because they view the 9-cluster solution as more meaningful than the 8-cluster solution. | Inappropriate metric for selection of ideal k in k-means (BIC).  Reliance on self-reported data. And no use of LMS data.  Small sample size. |
| Akpinar et al. (2020) | Sequence | Backtested on Historical Students | LMS/VLE/MOOC logs; Assessment/performance records; In-Class Behaviour | None Specified | 164 students - 1 Computer Science Course at Carnegie Melon University Spring 2019 | Clustering | Hierarchical Clustering; Latent Dirichlet Allocation | 9 Clusters:  • Cluster 1: Look at the lecture notes.  • Cluster 2: Look at the homework.  • Cluster 3: Looks at recitation materials. • Cluster 4: Catch up on the news.  • Cluster 5: Active homework engagement. • Cluster 6: Active homework engagement.  • Cluster 7: Library documentation. • Cluster 8: Tries Practice Exam.  • Cluster 9:  Look at the practice exam. | Authors use pattern mining to extract activity sequences. Similarity is measured by Jaro-Winkler distance. Cluster evaluation relies on the coherence score between sequences.  NLP approaches can be used to analyse students' learning strategies.  Activity among students increases before exams and homework.  Clusters generated are imbalanced, with the smallest clusters having around 2 or 3 candidate patterns.  Authors create a model that predicts assessment type from student interactions, showing distinct behaviours in homework and exams. | Needs generalizability with more data/ techniques. |
| Dermy & Brun (2020) | Sequence | Backtested on Historical Students | LMS/VLE/MOOC logs | None Specified | 113 first-year students - 1 Algorithms and Programming Course | Sequential Pattern Mining | PrefixSpan;  I-PrefixSpan | Nearly 99% of sequential patterns from PrefixScan were ignored after adding time intervals.   3 groups of activity patterns:   • Activities of close-in-time events: solving exercises, labs -common during exam prep. • Activities in hourly intervals, like exercise sheets lasting from 1 hour to a day, are noticeable when students delve deeper into a specific activity.  • Other intervals.  Linear Resolution Fitting ratio: 72% - daily resolution. | Activities at different temporal resolutions reveal that each has a unique rhythm, allowing monitoring of learner behaviour to see if it aligns with expectations.  The timing of the last student gap indicates the learner's next activity.  Provides insights into learning styles and optimal pathways. | Computational complexity.  Small sample size within the context of a single course.  Most behaviours do not have a stable timing, which led to the loss of more than 99% of all behavioural sequences.  Students don't always follow typical timing, causing irregularity and noise.  Temporal interval granularity may need refinement. |
| Jovanović et al. (2020) | Static Aggregate; Sequence | Backtested on Historical Students | LMS/VLE/MOOC logs; Assessment/performance records | None Specified | 969 students at an Australian University - 3 Editions of an online course | Clustering | Expectation-Maximization | 7 Clusters:  • Cluster 1 (MIX\_SIP\_LCA\_AS): Learning actions that are roughly equally distributed between passive social interaction (SIP), learning content access (LCA), and assessment (AS) actions. • Cluster 2 (MIX\_LCA\_A): Learning actions, with LCA and AS being more predominant. • Cluster 3 (LCA\_AS): Dominance LCA with notable presence of AS and limited monitoring (MM). • Cluster 4 (AS\_LCA\_M): Prevalence of AS, followed by LCA and MM actions. • Cluster 5 (AS\_INF\_MP\_LCA): Sessions focused on AS with presence of informing (INF), planning (MP) and LCA. • Cluster 6 (sh\_AS\_INF\_MM): Short (sh) sessions with dominance AS and presence of INF and MM.  • Cluster 7 (sh\_AS\_INF\_MP): sh with mostly AS but also INF MP actions. | Authors identify 7 "learning tactics," divided into 4 learning strategies.  Highlighting the need and benefit of explaining why a strategy is important and beneficial for the task.  Need to understand why students consider changing learning tactics during a course. | Multiple features share the same name, hindering interpretability.  Lack of insight into student motivation causes assumptions in log interpretation.  Need for generalisation to other courses and contexts. |
| Hierarchical Clustering | 4 Learning Strategies:  • Strategy A:  Active, regular, comprehensive - a learning tactic that mixes multiple forms of resources regularly • Strategy B: Active, Regular, Selective - high (lower than A) levels of activity - low presence of monitoring activities.  • Strategy C: Marginally active and regular, highly selective: low levels of activity, low activity and low regularity of study. When it comes to learning resources.  • Strategy D: Inactive, Irregular, Selective - Lower levels of activity than C. | The 4 clusters appear consistently over time.  Authors conclude that sticking to Strategy A or B yields the best results, while being consistent with a poor strategy leads to the worst performance.  Successful students use varied tactics and stay active, while the least successful reduce tactics over time. |

(2023) | Sequence | Backtested on Historical Students | LMS/VLE/MOOC logs | Complex Dynamic Systems Theory | 288 students - 1 First-Year Engineering Course | Clustering | Latent Class Growth Curve Modelling | This study groups behaviours and identifies how different student segments perform.  3 Clusters:  • Students with Stable Weekly Study Patterns (n=158): The entropy of recurrence in weekly activities stayed consistently high. • Students with Steep Chances in Weekly Study Patterns (n=40): Highest entropy of recurrence at the start of the course. (2025) | Sequence | Backtested on Historical Students | LMS/VLE/MOOC logs; Assessment/performance records | Self-Regulated Learning | 5870 students - 41 courses at a Dutch STEM university | Clustering | Expectation-Maximisation | 4 Learning Tactics:  • Assignment-Focused tactic: Most common tactic: Dominated by Assignment Pages and Mandatory Quizzes  • Material-Focused tactic: Over half of clicks are on materials, making it the second most common tactic. • Mixed Tactic: A broader variety of actions without a single dominant choice. • Quiz-Focused Tactic:  Students often choose to do mandatory or optional quizzes, but it is the least common tactic. | The initial clustering phase focused on learning tactics, followed by a second phase dedicated to learning strategies. The different learning tactics are the backbone of the learning strategies covered later. | Survivorship bias (only learners present over 2 years considered). | Process Mining | Markov Model | All strategies significantly impact grade, as shown by a Kruskall-Wallis test.  • Balanced Strategy:  No dominant tactic since all are used often. • Resource-Oriented:  Most actions involve accessing resources, making it the second-largest learning strategy and third in session count. • Grade-Oriented:  Most time is spent on grade menus. • Flexible: Adaptation to different courses, but not necessarily "balanced' engagement. | Different strategies recommend varying engagement with Surface and Deep learning approaches. The temporal granularity of analysis also affects how strategy transitions are analysed.  Results show that consistent learning strategy is more effective than changing strategy midway.  Authors analyse how learners change strategies during a course, often at midway points. Ultimately, a consistent deep-level learning approach is most effective, though switching strategies is better than always using surface-level approaches. |

(2024) | Sequence | Public Dataset | Question/answer sequences; Exercise/question/assignment metadata | 761 Biology students, 528 Law students, 383 Cardiff students, 382 Sydney19 learners and - Biology, Law & others | Previous graph models struggle with two issues:   1.
